\left\|\tilde{\bm{V}}^{-1}\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}\right>}\right)-\hat{{\bm{z}}}(t)\right\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\|\hat{{\bm{\delta}}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}}

(20)

for every sufficiently small
\alpha
. As
\tilde{\bm{V}}
are invertible, this directly implies (
7
).
∎

E.2
Proof for the Diagonal Case

Now we only need to prove
Theorem

E.1
. Let
{\bm{e}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{e}}_{d}
be the standard basis. Then
\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}=\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1}={\bm{e}}_{1}
in this diagonal case. We only use
{\bm{e}}_{1}
to stand for
\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}
and
\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1}
in the rest of our analysis.

Let
R>0
. Since
{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-smooth, there exists
\beta>0
such that

\|{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{h}})\|_{2}\leq\beta\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}

(21)

for all
\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{2},\|{\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{h}}\|_{2}\leq R
. Then the following can be proved by integration:

\displaystyle{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{h}})-{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{1}{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}+\xi{\bm{h}})\textup{{d}}\xi\right){\bm{h}},

(22)

\displaystyle\|{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}+{\bm{h}})-{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}){\bm{h}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2}.

(23)

By (
23
), we also have

\|{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}\|_{2}=\|{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})-{\bm{g}}({\bm{0}})-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}\|_{2}\leq\beta\|{\bm{\theta}}\|_{2}^{2}.

(24)

Let
\kappa:=\beta/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
. We assume WLOG that
R\leq 1/\kappa
. Let
F(x)=\log x-\log(1+\kappa x)
. It is easy to see that
F^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{x+\kappa x^{2}}
and
F(x)
is an increasing function with range
(-\infty,\log(1/\kappa))
. We use
F^{-1}(y)
to denote the inverse function of
F(x)
. Define
T_{\alpha}(r):=\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\left(F(r)-F(\alpha)\right)=\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\left(\log\frac{r}{\alpha}-\log\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\right)
.

Our proof only relies on the following properties of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
(besides that
\tilde{\mu}_{1},{\bm{e}}_{1}
are the top eigenvalue and eigenvector of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
):

Lemma E.2
.

For
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}):=\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\mu}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\mu}_{d})
, we have

1.

For any
{\bm{h}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{h}}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2}
;

2.

For any
t\geq 0
,
\left\|e^{t{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}=e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t}
.

Proof.

For Item 1,
{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{h}}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\tilde{\mu}_{i}h_{i}^{2}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2}
. For Item 2,
\left\|e^{t{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathrm{diag}(0,e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t},\dots,e^{\tilde{\mu}_{d}t})\right\|_{2}=e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t}
.
∎

Lemma E.3
.

For
{\bm{\theta}}(t)=\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t)
with
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2}\leq\alpha
and
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
,

\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\leq r.

Proof.

By (
24
) and
Lemma

E.2
, we have

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\textup{{d}}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}}{\textup{{d}}t}=\left<{\bm{\theta}}(t),{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))\right>\leq\left<{\bm{\theta}}(t),{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}(t)\right>+\beta\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{3}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}+\beta\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{3}.

This implies
\frac{\textup{{d}}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}}{\textup{{d}}t}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}(\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}+\kappa\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{2})
.
Since
F^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{x+\kappa x^{2}}
, we further have

\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}F(\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2})\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}.

So
F(\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2})\leq F(\alpha)+\tilde{\mu}_{1}t
. By definition of
T_{\alpha}(r)
, we then know that
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}\leq r
for all
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
. So

\log\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}\leq F(\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2})+\log(1+\kappa r)\leq F(\alpha)+\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\log(1+\kappa r).

Expending
F(\alpha)
proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma E.4
.

For
{\bm{\theta}}(t)=\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t)
with
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2}\leq\alpha
and
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
, we have

{\bm{\theta}}(t)=e^{t{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}{\bm{\theta}}_{0}+O(r^{2}).

Proof.

Let
\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)=e^{t{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left<{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t),{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\right>

\displaystyle=\left<{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}(t),{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\right>+({\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t))^{\top}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})({\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t))

\displaystyle\leq\|{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}\cdot\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}+\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}^{2},

where the last inequality is due to
Lemma

E.2
. By (
24
) and
Lemma

E.3
, we have

\|{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\beta\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\beta\left(\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\right)^{2}\cdot e^{2\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}.

So we have
\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\beta\left(\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\right)^{2}\cdot e^{2\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}+\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}
. By Grönwall’s inequality,

\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\beta\left(\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\right)^{2}\cdot e^{2\tilde{\mu}_{1}\tau}e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}(t-\tau)}d\tau.

Evaluating the integral gives

\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\beta\left(\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\right)^{2}e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\cdot\frac{e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}-1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\leq\kappa\left(\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\right)^{2}\leq\kappa r^{2},

which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma E.5
.

Let
{\bm{\theta}}(t)=\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t),\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)=\phi(\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{0},t)
. If
\max\{\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2},\|\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{0}\|_{2}\}\leq\alpha
, then for
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
,

\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\kappa r}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}_{0}\|_{2}.

Proof.

For
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
, by (
22
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left<{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-{\bm{g}}(\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)),{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\right>

\displaystyle=({\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t))^{\top}\left(\int_{0}^{1}{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}_{\xi}(t))\textup{{d}}\xi\right)({\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)),

where
{\bm{\theta}}_{\xi}(t):=\xi{\bm{\theta}}(t)+(1-\xi)\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)
. By
Lemma

E.3
,
\max\{\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)\|_{2},\|\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\}\leq\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}
for all
t\leq T_{\alpha}(r)
. So
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\xi}(t)\|_{2}\leq\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}
. Combining these with (
21
) and
Lemma

E.2
, we have

{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}_{\xi}(t)){\bm{h}}={\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}}){\bm{h}}+{\bm{h}}^{\top}({\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}}_{\xi}(t))-{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})){\bm{h}}\leq\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\beta\cdot\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\right)\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2},

for all
{\bm{h}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Thus,
\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\beta\cdot\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}\right)\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}
.
This implies

\displaystyle\log\frac{\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}}{\|{\bm{\theta}}(0)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(0)\|_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left(\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\beta\cdot\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}\tau}\right)d\tau

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\kappa\cdot\frac{1+\kappa r}{1+\kappa\alpha}\alpha e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\kappa r.

Therefore,
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(t)\|_{2}\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\kappa r}\|{\bm{\theta}}(0)-\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(0)\|_{2}
.
∎

Lemma E.6
.

For every
t\in(-\infty,+\infty)
,
{\bm{z}}(t)
exists and
{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)
converges to
{\bm{z}}(t)
in the following rate:

\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}=O(\alpha),

where
O
hides constants depending on
g({\bm{\theta}})
and
t
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma in the cases of
t\in(-\infty,F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
and
t>F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
respectively.

Case 1.

Fix
t\in(-\infty,F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}
be the unique number such that
\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{1+\kappa\tilde{\alpha}}=\alpha
(i.e.,
F(\tilde{\alpha})=\log\alpha
). Let
\alpha^{\prime}
be an arbitrary number less than
\alpha
. Let
t_{0}:=\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{\alpha}{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Then
t_{0}=\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\left(F(\tilde{\alpha})-\log\alpha^{\prime}\right)\leq T_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\tilde{\alpha})
. By
Lemma

E.4
, we have

\left\|\phi\left(\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1},t_{0}\right)-\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\phi\left(\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1},t_{0}\right)-e^{t_{0}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1}\right\|_{2}=O(\tilde{\alpha}^{2}).

Let
r:=F^{-1}(\tilde{\mu}_{1}t)\leq R
. Then
t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}=T_{\tilde{\alpha}}(r)
if
\tilde{\alpha}<r
.

By
Lemma

E.3
,
\left\|\phi\left(\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1},t_{0}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\tilde{\alpha}
. Also,
\|\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1}\|_{2}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{1+\kappa\tilde{\alpha}}\leq\tilde{\alpha}
. By
Lemma

E.5
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi\left(\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)-\phi\left(\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi\left(\phi(\alpha^{\prime}{\bm{e}}_{1},t_{0}),t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)-\phi\left(\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(\tilde{\alpha}^{2}\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}(t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha})+\kappa r})

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{\tilde{\alpha}^{2}}{\alpha}\right).

For
\alpha
small enough, we have
\tilde{\alpha}=O(\alpha)
, so for any
\alpha^{\prime}\in(0,\alpha)
,

\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)\right\|_{2}=O(\alpha).

This implies that
\{{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)\}
satisfies Cauchy’s criterion for every
t
, and thus the limit
{\bm{z}}(t)
exists for
t\leq F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
. The convergence rate can be deduced by taking limits for
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
on both sides.

Case 2.

For
t=F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tau
with
\tau>0
,
\phi({\bm{\theta}},\tau)
is locally Lipschitz with respect to
{\bm{\theta}}
. So

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi({\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}),\tau)-\phi({\bm{z}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}),\tau)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1})-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1})\right\|_{2})

\displaystyle=O(\alpha),

which proves the lemma for
t>F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
.
∎

Proof for
Theorem

E.1
.

The existence of
{\bm{z}}(t):=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\phi\left(\alpha{\bm{e}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)
has already been proved in
Lemma

E.6
, where we show
\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}=O(\alpha)
.

By the continuity of
\phi(\,\cdot\,,t)
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

{\bm{z}}(t)=\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\phi\left(\alpha\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)=\phi\left(\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\phi\left(\alpha\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha}\right),t\right)=\phi\left({\bm{z}}(0),t\right).

Now it is only left to prove (
7
). WLOG we can assume that
\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}
is decreasing and
\frac{\alpha}{2}\leq\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}\leq\alpha
(otherwise we can do reparameterization). Then our goal becomes proving

\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}=O\left(\alpha^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}}\right).

(25)

where
{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t):=\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},{\bm{e}}_{1}\right>}\right)
. We prove (
25
) in the cases of
t\in(-\infty,F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
and
t>F(R)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
respectively.

Case 1.

Fix
t\in(-\infty,(F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}=\alpha^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}}
. Let
\alpha_{1}:=e^{F(\tilde{\alpha}_{1})}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}{1+\kappa\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}
. Let
t_{0}:=\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}(F(\tilde{\alpha}_{1})-\log\alpha)\leq T_{\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}}(\tilde{\alpha}_{1})
. At time
t_{0}
, by
Lemma

E.2
we have

\left\|e^{t_{0}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t_{0}}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}=e^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}t_{0}}=e^{\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{2}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}(F(\tilde{\alpha}_{1})-\log\alpha)}=\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{2}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}}.

(26)

Let
q_{\alpha}:=\left<\frac{{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}}{\alpha},{\bm{e}}_{1}\right>
. By
Definition

5.2
, there exists
q>0
such that
q_{\alpha}\geq q
for all sufficiently small
\alpha
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t_{0}\right)-\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}{\bm{e}}_{1}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t_{0}\right)-e^{t_{0}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\right\|_{2}+\left\|\left(e^{t_{0}{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t_{0}}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right){\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2})+\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{\tilde{\mu}_{2}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}}\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{1}^{\tilde{\mu}_{2}/\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\alpha^{1-\tilde{\mu}_{2}/\tilde{\mu}_{1}})

\displaystyle=O(\alpha_{1}^{2}).

Let
r:=F^{-1}(\tilde{\mu}_{1}t+\log\frac{1}{q_{\alpha}})\leq R
. Then
t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}}=T_{\tilde{\alpha}}(r)
if
\tilde{\alpha}<r
. By
Lemma

E.3
,
\left\|\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t_{0}\right)\right\|_{2}\leq\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
. Also,
\|\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}{\bm{e}}_{1}\|_{2}\leq\alpha_{1}=\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}{1+\kappa\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}\leq\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
. By
Lemma

E.5
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha_{1}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\phi\left(\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t_{0}\right),t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}}\right)-\phi\left(\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}{\bm{e}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}}\right)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\alpha_{1}^{2}\cdot e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}\left(t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}q_{\alpha}}\right)+\kappa r}\right)

\displaystyle=O(\alpha_{1}).

Combining this with the convergence rate for
{\bm{z}}_{\alpha_{1}}(t)
, we have

\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}_{\alpha_{1}}(t)\right\|_{2}+\left\|{\bm{z}}_{\alpha_{1}}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}=O(\alpha_{1}).

Case 2.

For
t=(F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tau
with
\tau>0
,
\phi({\bm{\theta}},\tau)
is locally Lipschitz with respect to
{\bm{\theta}}
. So

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}(t)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}((F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}),\tau)-\phi({\bm{z}}((F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}),\tau)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle=O(\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\alpha}((F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1})-{\bm{z}}((F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1})\right\|_{2})

\displaystyle=O(\alpha_{1}),

which proves (
25
) for
t>(F(R)+\log q)/\tilde{\mu}_{1}
.
∎

E.3
Extension to Non-Diagonalizable Case

The proof in
Section

E.2
can be generalized to the case where
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
. Now we state the theorem formally and sketch the proof idea. We use the notations
{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}}),\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t),{\bm{J}}({\bm{\theta}})
as in
Section

5.1
, but we do not assume that
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is diagonalizable. Instead, we use
\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2},\dots,\tilde{\mu}_{d}\in\mathbb{C}
to denote the eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
, repeated according to algebraic multiplicity. We sort the eigenvalues in the descending order of the real part of each eigenvalue, i.e.,
\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{1})\geq\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2})\geq\cdots\geq\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{d})
, where
\Re(z)
stands for the real part of a complex number
z\in\mathbb{C}
. We call the eigenvalue with the largest real part the top eigenvalue.

Theorem E.7
.

Assume that
{\bm{\theta}}={\bm{0}}
is a critical point and the following regularity conditions hold:

1.

{\bm{g}}({\bm{\theta}})
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-smooth;

2.

\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t)
exists for all
{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
and
t
;

3.

The top eigenvalue of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is unique and is a positive real number, i.e.,

\tilde{\mu}_{1}>\max\{\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2}),0\}.

Let
\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}
be the left and right eigenvectors associated with
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
, satisfying
\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}^{\top}\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1}=1
. Let
{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t):=\phi(\alpha\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},t+\frac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\alpha})
for every
\alpha>0
, then
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
,
{\bm{z}}(t):=\lim\limits_{\alpha\to 0}{\bm{z}}_{\alpha}(t)
exists and
{\bm{z}}(t)=\phi({\bm{z}}(0),t)
. If
{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}
converges to
{\bm{0}}
with positive alignment with
\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}
as
\alpha\to 0
, then for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and for any
\epsilon>0
, there is a constant
C>0
such that for every sufficiently small
\alpha
,

\left\|\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\tfrac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\tfrac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}\right>}\right)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}-\epsilon},

(27)

where
\tilde{\gamma}:=\tilde{\mu}_{1}-\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2})
is the eigenvalue gap.

Proof Sketch.

Define the following two types of matrices. For
r\geq 1,a,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,\delta}:=\begin{bmatrix}a&\delta&&&&\\
&a&\delta&&&\\
&&a&\delta&&\\
&&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&&a&\delta\\
&&&&&a\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}.

For
r\geq 1,a,b,\delta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,\delta}:=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{C}}&\delta{\bm{I}}&&&&\\
&{\bm{C}}&\delta{\bm{I}}&&&\\
&&{\bm{C}}&\delta{\bm{I}}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&&{\bm{C}}&\delta{\bm{I}}\\
&&&&&{\bm{C}}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{2r\times 2r},

where
{\bm{C}}=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}a&-b\\
b&a\end{smallmatrix}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
.

By linear algebra, the real matrix
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
can be written in the real Jordan normal form, i.e.,
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})=\tilde{\bm{V}}\mathrm{diag}({\bm{J}}_{[1]},\dots,{\bm{J}}_{[m]})\tilde{\bm{V}}^{-1}
, where
\tilde{\bm{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is an invertible matrix, and each
{\bm{J}}_{[j]}
is a real Jordan block. Recall that there are two types of real Jordan blocks,
{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,1}
or
{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,1}
. The former one is associated with a real eigenvalue
a
, and the latter one is associated with a pair of complex eigenvalues
a\pm bi
. The sum of sizes of all Jordan blocks corresponding to a real eigenvalue
a
is its algebraic multiplicity. The sum of sizes of all Jordan blocks corresponding to a pair of complex eigenvalues
a\pm bi
is two times the algebraic multiplicity of
a+bi
or
a-bi
(note that
a\pm bi
have the same multiplicity).

It is easy to see that
{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,\delta}={\bm{D}}{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,1}{\bm{D}}^{-1}
for
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}(\delta^{r},\delta^{r-1},\dots,\delta)\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
and
{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,\delta}={\bm{D}}{\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,1}{\bm{D}}^{-1}
for
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}(\delta^{r},\delta^{r},\delta^{r-1},\delta^{r-1},\dots,\delta,\delta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2r\times 2r}
. This means for every
\delta>0
there exists
\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}
such that
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})=\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}{\bm{J}}_{\delta}\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}
, where
{\bm{J}}_{\delta}:=\mathrm{diag}({\bm{J}}_{\delta[1]},\dots,{\bm{J}}_{\delta[m]})
,
{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}:={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,\delta}
if
{\bm{J}}_{[j]}:={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,1}
, or
{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}:={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,\delta}
if
{\bm{J}}_{[j]}:={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,b,1}
. Since the top eigenvalue of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is positive and unique,
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
corresponds to only one block
[\tilde{\mu}_{1}]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 1}
. WLOG we let
{\bm{J}}_{1}=[\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
, and thus
{\bm{J}}_{\delta[1]}=[\tilde{\mu}_{1}]
.

We only need to select a parameter
\delta>0
and prove the theorem in the case of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})={\bm{J}}_{\delta}
since we can change the basis in a similar way as we have done in
Section

E.1
. By scrutinizing the proof for
Theorem

E.1
, we can find that we only need to reprove
Lemma

E.2
. However,
Lemma

E.2
may not be correct since
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is not diagonal anymore. Instead, we prove the following:

1.

If
\delta\in(0,\tilde{\gamma})
, then
{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}_{\delta}{\bm{h}}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2}
for all
{\bm{h}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

2.

For any
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}\in(\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2}),\tilde{\mu}_{1})
, if
\delta\in(0,\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}-\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2}))
, then
\left\|e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta}}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}t}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Proof for Item 1.

Let
{\mathcal{K}}
be the set of pairs
(k_{1},k_{2})
such that
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
and the entry of
{\bm{J}}_{\delta}
at the
k_{1}
-th row and the
k_{2}
-th column is non-zero. Then we have

\displaystyle{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}_{\delta}{\bm{h}}={\bm{h}}^{\top}\frac{{\bm{J}}_{\delta}+{\bm{J}}_{\delta}^{\top}}{2}{\bm{h}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{k})h_{k}^{2}+\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathcal{K}}}h_{k_{1}}h_{k_{2}}\delta

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{k})h_{k}^{2}+\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathcal{K}}}\frac{h^{2}_{k_{1}}+h^{2}_{k_{2}}}{2}\delta.

Note that
\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{k})\leq\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2})
for
k\geq 2
. Also note that there is no pair in
{\mathcal{K}}
has
k_{1}=1
or
k_{2}=1
, and for every
k\geq 2
there are at most two pairs in
{\mathcal{K}}
has
k_{1}=k
or
k_{2}=k
. Combining all these together gives

{\bm{h}}^{\top}{\bm{J}}_{\delta}{\bm{h}}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}h_{1}^{2}+(\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2})+\delta)\sum_{k=2}^{d}h_{k}^{2}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{1}\|{\bm{h}}\|_{2}^{2},

which proves Item 1.

Proof for Item 2.

Since
{\bm{J}}_{\delta}
is block diagonal, we only need to prove that
\|e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}}\|_{2}\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}t}
for every
j\geq 2
. If
{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,\delta}=a{\bm{I}}+\delta{\bm{N}}
, where
{\bm{N}}
is the nilpotent matrix, then

e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}}=e^{at{\bm{I}}+\delta t{\bm{N}}}=e^{at{\bm{I}}}e^{\delta t{\bm{N}}}=e^{at}e^{\delta t{\bm{N}}},

where the second equality uses the fact that
{\bm{I}}
and
{\bm{N}}
are commutable. So we have

\|e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}}\|_{2}\leq e^{at}\|e^{\delta t{\bm{N}}}\|_{2}=e^{at}e^{\delta t\|{\bm{N}}\|_{2}}\leq e^{(a+\delta)t}.

If
{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}={\bm{J}}^{(r)}_{a,\delta}={\bm{D}}+\delta{\bm{N}}^{2}
, where
{\bm{D}}=\mathrm{diag}({\bm{C}},{\bm{C}},\dots,{\bm{C}})
and
{\bm{N}}
is the nilpotent matrix, then

e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}}=e^{t{\bm{D}}+\delta t{\bm{N}}^{2}}=e^{t{\bm{D}}}e^{\delta t{\bm{N}}^{2}},

where the second equality uses the fact that
{\bm{D}}
and
{\bm{N}}^{2}
are commutable. Note that
e^{t{\bm{C}}}=e^{at}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\cos(bt)&-\sin(bt)\\
\sin(bt)&\cos(bt)\end{smallmatrix}\right]
, which implies
\|e^{t{\bm{D}}}\|_{2}=\|e^{t{\bm{C}}}\|_{2}=e^{at}
. So we have

\|e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta[j]}}\|_{2}\leq\|e^{t{\bm{D}}}\|_{2}\cdot\|e^{\delta t{\bm{N}}^{2}}\|_{2}=e^{at}e^{\delta t\|{\bm{N}}^{2}\|_{2}}\leq e^{(a+\delta)t}.

Since
\delta\in(0,\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}-\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2}))
, we know that
a+\delta<\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}
, which completes the proof.

Proof for a fixed
\delta
.

Since Item 1 continues to hold for
\delta\in(0,\tilde{\gamma})
,
Lemmas

E.3
,
E.4
,
E.5
and
E.6
also hold. This proves that
{\bm{z}}(t)
exists and satisfies (
6
).

It remains to prove (
27
) for any
\epsilon>0
. Let
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}\in(0,\tilde{\gamma})
be a number such that
\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}-\epsilon
. Fix
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}=\tilde{\mu}_{1}-\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime},\delta=\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}-\Re(\tilde{\mu}_{2})
.
By Item 2, we have
\left\|e^{t{\bm{J}}_{\delta}}-e^{\tilde{\mu}_{1}t}{\bm{e}}_{1}{\bm{e}}_{1}^{\top}\right\|_{2}\leq e^{\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}t}
for all
t\geq 0
. By scrutinizing the proof for
Theorem

E.1
, we can find that the only place we use Item 2 in
Lemma

E.2
is in (
26
). For proving (
27
), we can repeat the proof while replacing all the occurrences of
\tilde{\mu}_{2}
by
\tilde{\mu}^{\prime}_{2}
. Then we know that for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there is a constant
C>0
such that

\left\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\tfrac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\tfrac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}\right>}\right)-\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}\leq C\cdot\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}},

for every sufficiently small
\alpha
. By definition of
\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}
,
\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}\geq\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}-\epsilon
. Since
{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\to{\bm{0}}
as
\alpha\to 0
, we have
\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}<1
for sufficiently small
\alpha
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\tfrac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\tfrac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}\right>}\right)-{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}\phi\left({\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},t+\tfrac{1}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}}\log\tfrac{1}{\left<{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha},\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}\right>}\right)-\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}{\bm{z}}(t)\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}\|_{2}\cdot C\cdot\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}\|_{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}}\cdot\|{\bm{\delta}}_{\alpha}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}-\epsilon}.

Absorbing
\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}\|_{2}\cdot\|\tilde{\bm{V}}_{\delta}^{-1}\|_{2}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}{\tilde{\mu}_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}^{\prime}}}
into
C
proves (
27
).
∎

Appendix F
Eigenvalues of Jacobians and Hessians

In this section we analyze the eigenvalues of the Jacobian
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}})
at critical points of (
2
).

For notation simplicity, we write
\mathrm{sz}({\bm{A}}):={\bm{A}}+{\bm{A}}^{\top}
to denote the symmetric matrix produced by adding up
{\bm{A}}
and its transpose, and write
\mathrm{ac}\{{\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}\}={\bm{A}}{\bm{B}}+{\bm{B}}{\bm{A}}
to denote the anticommutator of two matrices
{\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}
. Then
{\bm{g}}({\bm{W}})
can be written as
{\bm{g}}({\bm{W}}):=-\mathrm{ac}\{\nabla f({\bm{W}}),{\bm{W}}\}
.

Let
{\bm{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
be a stationary point of the function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to\mathbb{R},\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}})=\frac{1}{2}f({\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top})
, i.e.,
\nabla\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})=\nabla f({\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}){\bm{U}}_{0}={\bm{0}}
. By
Lemma

A.1
, this implies

\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{W}}_{0}={\bm{0}}

(28)

for
{\bm{W}}_{0}:={\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}
, and thus
{\bm{W}}_{0}
is a critical point of (
2
).

For a real-valued or vector-valued function
F({\bm{\theta}})
, we use
DF({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\delta}}],D^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\delta}}_{1},{\bm{\delta}}_{2}]
to denote the first- and second-order directional derivatives of
F(\,\cdot\,)
at
{\bm{\theta}}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a linear space, which can be
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. For a function
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
, we use
DF({\bm{\theta}})
to denote the directional derivative of
F
at
{\bm{\theta}}
, represented by the linear operator

DF({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X},{\bm{\Delta}}\mapsto DF({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{F({\bm{\theta}}+t{\bm{\Delta}})-F({\bm{\theta}})}{t}.

We also write
DF({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]:=\left<DF({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1}],{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}\right>
.

For a function
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
, we use
D^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})=D(\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}))
to denote the second directional derivative of
F
at
{\bm{\theta}}
, i.e.,
D^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]=D(\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}))[{\bm{\Delta}}],D^{2}F({\bm{\theta}})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]=D(\nabla F({\bm{\theta}}))[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]
.

Define
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}):=D{\bm{g}}({\bm{W}})
. By simple calculus, we can compute the formula for
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})
:

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{ac}\{\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),{\bm{\Delta}}\}-\mathrm{ac}\{D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}],{\bm{W}}_{0}\},

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\left<\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}_{1}{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}^{\top})\right>-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},\mathrm{sz}({\bm{W}}_{0}{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}^{\top})],

where
{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.

We can also compute the formula for
D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})
:

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{\Delta}}+D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top})]{\bm{U}}_{0},

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left<\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}_{1}{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}^{\top})\right>+D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}_{1}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}),\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}_{2}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top})]\right),

where
{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
.

F.1
Eigenvalues at the Origin

The eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is given in
Lemma

5.4
. Now we provide the proof.

Proof for
Lemma

5.4
.

For
{\bm{W}}_{0}={\bm{0}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=-\nabla f({\bm{0}}){\bm{\Delta}}-{\bm{\Delta}}\nabla f({\bm{0}})

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}_{1},{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}]

\displaystyle=-\left<\nabla f({\bm{0}}),\mathrm{sz}({\bm{\Delta}}_{1}{\bm{\Delta}}_{2}^{\top})\right>

It is easy to see from the second equation that
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
is symmetric.

Let
-\nabla f({\bm{0}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}
be the eigendecomposition of the symmetric matrix
-\nabla f({\bm{0}})
. Then we have

\displaystyle{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}\left({\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}{\bm{\Delta}}+{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mu_{i}\left({\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}){\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j})\left<{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}\right>{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top},

which proves (
8
).

For
{\bm{\Delta}}={\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}
, we have

{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]=(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}){\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}){\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}=(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}){\bm{\Delta}}.

So
{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}
is an eigenvector of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
associated with eigenvalue
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}
. Note that
\{{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top}:i,j\in[d]\}
spans all the symmetric matrices, so these are all the eigenvectors in the space of symmetric matrices.

For every antisymmetric matrix
{\bm{\Delta}}
(i.e.,
{\bm{\Delta}}=-{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}
), we have

{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]={\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}]={\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[-{\bm{\Delta}}].

So
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})[{\bm{\Delta}}]={\bm{0}}
and every antisymmetric matrix is an eigenvector associated with eigenvalue
0
.

Since every matrix can be expressed as the sum of a symmetric matrix and an antisymmetric matrix, we have found all the eigenvalues.
∎

F.2
Eigenvalues at Second-Order Stationary Points

Now we study the eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})
when
{\bm{U}}_{0}
is a second-order stationary point of
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
, i.e.,
\nabla\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})={\bm{0}},D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{\Delta}}]\geq{\bm{0}}
for all
{\bm{\Delta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. We further assume that
{\bm{U}}_{0}
is full-rank, i.e.,
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{U}}_{0})=r
. This condition is meet if
{\bm{W}}_{0}:={\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{T}
is a local minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
but not a minimizer in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
.

Lemma F.1
.

For
r\leq d
, if
{\bm{U}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
is a second-order stationary point of
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
, then either
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{U}}_{0})=\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}_{0})=r
, or
{\bm{W}}_{0}
is a minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
, where
{\bm{W}}_{0}={\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}
.

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that
{\bm{U}}_{0}
has rank
<r
and
{\bm{W}}_{0}
is a minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
. The former one implies that there exists a unit vector
{\bm{q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
such that
{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{q}}={\bm{0}}
, and the latter one implies that there exists
{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
{\bm{v}}^{\top}\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{v}}<0
by
Lemma

A.2
.

Let
{\bm{\Delta}}={\bm{v}}{\bm{q}}^{\top}
. Then we have

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=\left<\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\right>+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\mathrm{sz}({\bm{v}}({\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{q}})^{\top}),\mathrm{sz}({\bm{v}}({\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{q}})^{\top})]

\displaystyle=\left<\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\right>+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{0}},{\bm{0}}]

\displaystyle=\left<\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\right>.

So
D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{\Delta}}]<0
, which leads to a contradiction.
∎

By (
28
), the symmetric matrices
-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0})
and
{\bm{W}}_{0}
commute, so they can be simultaneously diagonalizable. Since (
28
) also implies that they have different column spans, we can have the following diagonalization:

-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-r}\mu_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top},\qquad{\bm{W}}_{0}=\sum_{i=d-r+1}^{d}\mu_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top}.

(29)

First we prove the following lemma on the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the linear operator
-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})
:

Lemma F.2
.

For every
{\bm{\Delta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, if

{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}+{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}={\bm{0}}

(30)

then
{\bm{\Delta}}
is an eigenvector of the linear operator
-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[\,\cdot\,]:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
associated with eigenvalue
0
. Moreover, the solutions of (
30
) spans a linear space of dimension
\frac{r(r-1)}{2}
.

Proof.

Suppose
{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}+{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}={\bm{0}}
. Then we have
{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}=-{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}
, and thus
{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}=-{\bm{U}}_{0}^{+}{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}
, where
{\bm{U}}_{0}^{+}
is the pseudoinverse of the full-rank matrix
{\bm{U}}_{0}
. This implies that there is a matrix
{\bm{R}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
, such that
{\bm{\Delta}}={\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{R}}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]

\displaystyle=-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{R}}-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}+{\bm{\Delta}}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}]{\bm{U}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\left(\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{U}}_{0}\right){\bm{R}}-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{0}}]{\bm{U}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\bm{0}}.

Replacing
{\bm{\Delta}}
with
{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{R}}
in (
30
) gives
{\bm{U}}_{0}({\bm{R}}+{\bm{R}}^{\top}){\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}={\bm{0}}
, which is equivalent to
{\bm{R}}=-{\bm{R}}^{\top}
since
{\bm{U}}_{0}
is full-rank. Since the dimension of
r\times r
antisymmetric matrices is
\frac{r(r-1)}{2}
, the span spanned by the solutions of (
30
) also has dimension
\frac{r(r-1)}{2}
.
∎

Definition F.3

(Eigendecomposition of
-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})
)
.

Let

-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]=\sum_{p=1}^{rd}\xi_{p}\left<{\bm{E}}_{p},{\bm{\Delta}}\right>{\bm{E}}_{p}

be the eigendecomposition of the symmetric linear operator
-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[\,\cdot\,]:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
, where
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{rd}\in\mathbb{R}
are eigenvalues,
{\bm{E}}_{1},\dots,{\bm{E}}_{rd}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
are eigenvectors satisfying
\left<{\bm{E}}_{p},{\bm{E}}_{q}\right>=\delta_{pq}
. We enforce
\xi_{p}
to be
0
and
{\bm{E}}_{p}
to be a solution of (
30
) for every
rd-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}<p\leq rd
.

Lemma F.4
.

Let
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
be a matrix. If
\{\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{1},\dots,\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{K}\}
is a set of linearly independent left eigenvectors associated with eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda}_{K}
and
\{\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},\dots,\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{D-K}\}
is a set of linearly independent right eigenvectors associated with eigenvalues
\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}_{D-K}
, and
\left<\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{i},\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{j}\right>=0
for all
1\leq i\leq K,1\leq j\leq D-K
, then
\hat{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda}_{K},\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}_{D-K}
are all the eigenvalues of
{\bm{A}}
.

Proof.

Let
\hat{\bm{U}}:=(\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{1},\dots,\hat{{\bm{u}}}_{K})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K\times D}
and
\tilde{\bm{V}}:=(\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1},\dots,\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{D-K})\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times(D-K)}
. Then both
\hat{\bm{U}}
and
\tilde{\bm{V}}
are full-rank. Let
\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}=\hat{\bm{U}}^{\top}(\hat{\bm{U}}\hat{\bm{U}}^{\top})^{-1},\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}=(\tilde{\bm{V}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{V}})^{-1}\tilde{\bm{V}}^{\top}
be the pseudoinverses of
\hat{\bm{U}}
and
\tilde{\bm{V}}
.

Now we define

{\bm{P}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{U}}\\
\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}\end{bmatrix},\quad{\bm{Q}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}&\tilde{\bm{V}}\end{bmatrix}.

Then we have

{\bm{P}}{\bm{Q}}=\begin{bmatrix}\hat{\bm{U}}\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}&\hat{\bm{U}}\tilde{\bm{V}}\\
\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}&\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}\tilde{\bm{V}}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that
\hat{\bm{U}}\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}={\bm{I}}_{K}
,
\hat{\bm{U}}\tilde{\bm{V}}={\bm{0}}
,
\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}\hat{\bm{U}}^{+}=(\tilde{\bm{V}}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{V}})^{-1}(\hat{\bm{U}}\tilde{\bm{V}})^{\top}(\hat{\bm{U}}\hat{\bm{U}}^{\top})^{-1}={\bm{0}}
,
\tilde{\bm{V}}^{+}\tilde{\bm{V}}={\bm{I}}_{D-K}
. So
{\bm{P}}{\bm{Q}}={\bm{I}}_{D}
, or equivalently
{\bm{Q}}={\bm{P}}^{-1}
. Then we have

{\bm{P}}^{-1}{\bm{A}}{\bm{P}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathrm{diag}(\hat{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda}_{K})&*\\
{\bm{0}}&\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}_{D-K})\end{bmatrix},

where
*
can be any
K\times(D-K)
matrix. Since
{\bm{P}}^{-1}{\bm{A}}{\bm{P}}
is upper-triangular, we know that
{\bm{P}}^{-1}{\bm{A}}{\bm{P}}
has eigenvalues
\hat{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\hat{\lambda}_{K},\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}_{D-K}
, and so does
{\bm{A}}
.
∎

Theorem F.5
.

The eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})
can be fully classified into the following
3
types:

1.

\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}
is an eigenvalue for every
1\leq i\leq j\leq d-r
, and
\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}:={\bm{v}}_{i}{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\top}+{\bm{v}}_{j}{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top}
is an associated left eigenvector.

2.

\xi_{p}
is an eigenvalue for every
1\leq p\leq rd-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}
, and
\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}:={\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}+{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{E}}_{p}^{\top}
is an associated right eigenvector.

3.

0
is an eigenvalue, and any antisymmetric matrix is an associated right eigenvector, which spans a linear space of dimension
\frac{d(d-1)}{2}
.

Proof of
Theorem

F.5
.

We first prove each item respectively, and then prove that these are all the eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})
.

Proof for Item 1.

For
\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}={\bm{v}}_{i}{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\top}+{\bm{v}}_{j}{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top}
, it is easy to check:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ac}\{-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}),\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}\}

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}{\bm{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle={\bm{0}}

\displaystyle{\bm{W}}_{0}\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}

\displaystyle={\bm{0}}

So we have

{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}},\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}]=(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})\left<{\bm{\Delta}},\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}\right>-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}},{\bm{0}}]=(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j})\left<{\bm{\Delta}},\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}\right>,

which shows that
\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}
is a left eigenvector associated with eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}
.

Proof for Item 2.

By definition of eigenvector, we have
-D^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}}_{0})[{\bm{E}}_{p}]=\xi_{p}{\bm{E}}_{p}
, so

\xi_{p}{\bm{E}}_{p}=-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{E}}_{p}-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{E}}_{p}^{\top}+{\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}]{\bm{U}}_{0}.

Right-multiplying both sides by
{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}
, we get

\displaystyle\xi_{p}{\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}

\displaystyle=-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}]{\bm{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0})({\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}+{\bm{U}}_{0}{\bm{E}}_{p}^{\top})-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}]{\bm{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0})\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}-D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}]{\bm{W}}_{0},

where the second equality uses the fact that
\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0}){\bm{U}}_{0}={\bm{0}}
since
{\bm{U}}_{0}
is a critical point. Taking both sides into
\mathrm{sz}(\cdot)
gives

\displaystyle\xi_{p}\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{sz}(\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{0})\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p})-\mathrm{sz}(D^{2}f({\bm{W}}_{0})[\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}]{\bm{W}}_{0})

\displaystyle={\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}],

which proves that
\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}
is a right eigenvector associated with eigenvalue
\xi_{p}
.

Proof for Item 3.

Since
\nabla f({\bm{W}})
is symmetric,
{\bm{g}}({\bm{W}})
is also symmetric. For any
{\bm{\Delta}}=-{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}
,

{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]={\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}^{\top}]={\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[-{\bm{\Delta}}].

So
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]={\bm{0}}
and
{\bm{\Delta}}
is an eigenvector associated with eigenvalue
0
.

No other eigenvalues.

Let
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
be the space of symmetric matrices and
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
be the space of antisymmetric matrices. It is easy to see that
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
are orthogonal to each other, and
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
and
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
are invariant subspaces of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]
. Let
{\bm{h}}:{\mathbb{S}}_{d}\to{\mathbb{S}}_{d},{\bm{\Delta}}\mapsto{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]
be the linear operator
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})[{\bm{\Delta}}]
restricted on symmetric matrices. We only need to prove that
{\bm{h}}
is diagonalizable.

It is easy to see that
\{\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij}\}
are linearly independent to each other and thus spans a subspace of
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
with dimension
\frac{(d-r)(d-r+1)}{2}
. We can also prove that
\{\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}\}
spans a subspace of
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
with dimension
rd-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}
by contradiction. Assume to the contrary that there exists scalars
\alpha_{p}
for
1\leq p\leq rd-r(r-1)/2
, not all zero, such that
\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2}\alpha_{p}\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}={\bm{0}}
. Then
\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2}\alpha_{p}{\bm{E}}_{p}
is a solution of (
30
). However, this suggests that
\sum_{p=1}^{rd-r(r-1)/2}\alpha_{p}{\bm{E}}_{p}
lies in the span of
\{{\bm{E}}_{p}\}_{rd-r(r-1)/2<p\leq rd}
, which contradicts to the linear independence of
\{{\bm{E}}_{p}\}_{1\leq p\leq rd}
.

Note that

\frac{(d-r)(d-r+1)}{2}+\left(rd-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\right)=\frac{d(d+1)}{2}=\dim({\mathbb{S}}_{d}).

Also note that
\left<\hat{{\bm{U}}}_{ij},\tilde{{\bm{V}}}_{p}\right>=2{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{j}+2{\bm{v}}_{j}^{\top}{\bm{E}}_{p}{\bm{U}}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{v}}_{i}=0
. By
Lemma

F.4
, Items 1 and 2 give all the eigenvalues of
{\bm{h}}
, and thus Items 1, 2, 3 give all the eigenvalues of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{W}}_{0})
.
∎

Appendix G
Proofs for the Depth-2 Case

G.1
Proof for
Theorem

5.6

Proof for
Theorem

5.6
.

Since
{\bm{W}}(t)
is always symmetric, it suffices to study the dynamics of the lower triangle of
{\bm{W}}(t)
. For any symmetric matrix
{\bm{W}}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
, let
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}})\in\mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d+1)}{2}}
be the vector consisting of the
\frac{d(d+1)}{2}
entries of
{\bm{W}}
in the lower triangle, permuted according to some fixed order.

Let
{\bm{g}}({\bm{W}})
be the function defined in (
2
), which always maps symmetric matrices to symmetric matrices. Let
\tilde{{\bm{g}}}:\mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d+1)}{2}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\frac{d(d+1)}{2}}
be the function such that
\tilde{{\bm{g}}}(\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}))=\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{g}}({\bm{W}}))
for any
{\bm{W}}\in{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
. For
{\bm{W}}(t)
evolving with (
2
), we view
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))
as a dynamical system.

\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))=\tilde{{\bm{g}}}(\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))).

By
Lemma

5.4
, the spaces of symmetric matrices
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
and antisymmetric matrices
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
are invariant subspaces of
{\bm{J}}({\bm{0}})
, and
\left\{(\mu_{i}+\mu_{j},{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}^{\top}+{\bm{u}}_{1[j]}{\bm{u}}_{1[i]}^{\top})\right\}_{1\leq i\leq j\leq d}
is the set of all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors in the invariant subspace
{\mathbb{S}}_{d}
. Thus,
\tilde{\mu}_{1}:=2\mu_{1}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{2}:=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
are the largest and second largest eigenvalues of the Jacobian of
\tilde{{\bm{g}}}(\cdot)
at
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}})={\bm{0}}
, and
\tilde{{\bm{u}}}_{1}=\tilde{{\bm{v}}}_{1}={\bm{u}}_{1}{\bm{u}}_{1}^{\top}
are the corresponding left and right eigenvectors of the top eigenvalue. Then it is easy to translate
Theorem

5.3
to
Theorem

5.6
.
∎

G.2
Proof for
Theorem

5.8

The proof for
Theorem

5.8
relies on the following Lemma on the gradient flow around a local minimizer:

Lemma G.1
.

If
\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}
is a local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}})
and for all
\|{\bm{\theta}}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq r
,
{\bm{\theta}}
satisfies Łojasiewicz inequality:

\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}})\right\|_{2}\geq c\left(\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}})-\mathcal{L}(\bar{{\bm{\theta}}})\right)^{\mu}

for some
\mu\in[1/2,1)
, then the gradient flow
{\bm{\theta}}(t)=\phi({\bm{\theta}}_{0},t)
converges to a point
{\bm{\theta}}_{\infty}
near
\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}
if
{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
is close enough to
\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}
, and the distance can be bounded by
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\infty}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}=O(\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2(1-\mu)})
.

Proof.

For every
t\geq 0
, if
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq r
,

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\left(\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-\mathcal{L}(\bar{{\bm{\theta}}})\right)^{1-\mu}

\displaystyle=(1-\mu)\left(\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-\mathcal{L}(\bar{{\bm{\theta}}})\right)^{-\mu}\cdot\left<\nabla\mathcal{L},\frac{d{\bm{\theta}}}{dt}\right>

\displaystyle=-(1-\mu)\left(\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}}(t))-\mathcal{L}(\bar{{\bm{\theta}}})\right)^{-\mu}\cdot\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}\right\|_{2}\cdot\left\|\frac{d{\bm{\theta}}}{dt}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq-(1-\mu)c\left\|\frac{d{\bm{\theta}}}{dt}\right\|_{2}.

Therefore,
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\left\|\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{\theta}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\right\|_{2}\textup{{d}}t\leq\frac{1}{(1-\mu)c}\mathcal{L}({\bm{\theta}}_{0})^{1-\mu}=O(\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2(1-\mu)})
. If we choose
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}
small enough, then
\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}\leq\|{\bm{\theta}}(t)-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}\|_{2}+\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}=O(\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2(1-\mu)})<r
, and thus
\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left\|\frac{d{\bm{\theta}}}{dt}\right\|_{2}\textup{{d}}t
is convergent and finite. This implies that
{\bm{\theta}}_{\infty}:=\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\bm{\theta}}(t)
exists and
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{\infty}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}=O(\|{\bm{\theta}}_{0}-\bar{{\bm{\theta}}}\|_{2}^{2(1-\mu)})
.
∎

Proof for
Theorem

5.8
.

Since
{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{1}(t)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d,\leq 1}
satisfies (
2
), there exists
{\bm{u}}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
{\bm{u}}(t){\bm{u}}(t)^{\top}={\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{1}(t)
and
{\bm{u}}(t)
satisfies (
1
), i.e.,
\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{u}}}{\textup{{d}}t}=-\nabla\mathcal{L}({\bm{u}})
, where
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R},{\bm{u}}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}f({\bm{u}}{\bm{u}}^{\top})
. If
{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{1}(t)
does not diverge to infinity, then so does
{\bm{u}}(t)
. This implies that there is a limit point
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
of the set
\{{\bm{u}}(t):t\geq 0\}
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}:=\{{\bm{u}}:\mathcal{L}({\bm{u}})\geq\mathcal{L}(\bar{{\bm{u}}})\}
. Since
\mathcal{L}({\bm{u}}(t))
is non-increasing, we have
{\bm{u}}(t)\in{\mathcal{U}}
for all
t
. Note that
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
is a local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathcal{U}}
. By analyticity of
f(\,\cdot\,)
, Łojasiewicz inequality holds for
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
around
\bar{{\bm{u}}}

[
Łojasiewicz 1965
]
. Applying
Lemma

G.1
for
\mathcal{L}
restricted on
{\mathcal{U}}
, we know that if
{\bm{u}}(t_{0})
is sufficiently close to
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
, the remaining length of the trajectory of
{\bm{u}}(t)
(
t\geq t_{0}
) is finite and thus
\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\bm{u}}(t)
exists. As
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
is a limit point, this limit can only be
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
. Therefore,
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{1}:=\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{1}(t)=\bar{{\bm{u}}}\bar{{\bm{u}}}^{\top}
exists.

If
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{1}
is a minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
,
\overline{{\bm{U}}}=(\bar{{\bm{u}}},{\bm{0}},\cdots,{\bm{0}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is also a minimizer of
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R},{\bm{U}}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}f({\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top})
. By analyticity of
f(\,\cdot\,)
, Łojasiewicz inequality holds for
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
around
\overline{{\bm{U}}}
. For every
\epsilon>0
, we can always find a time
t_{\epsilon}
such that
\|{\bm{u}}(t_{\epsilon})-\bar{{\bm{u}}}\|_{2}\leq\epsilon/2
. On the other hand, by
Theorem

5.6
, there exists a number
\alpha_{\epsilon}
such that for every
\alpha<\alpha_{\epsilon}
,

\left\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T({\bm{W}}_{\alpha})+t_{\epsilon})-{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{1}(t_{\epsilon})\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon/2,\quad\text{where}\quad T({\bm{W}}):=\frac{1}{2\mu_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\left<{\bm{W}},{\bm{u}}_{1}{\bm{u}}_{1}^{\top}\right>}.

Combining these together we have
\left\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T({\bm{W}}_{\alpha})+t_{\epsilon})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{1}\right\|_{2}\leq\epsilon
.

It is easy to construct a factorization
\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T({\bm{W}}_{\alpha})+t_{\epsilon}):={\bm{U}}_{\alpha,\epsilon}{\bm{U}}_{\alpha,\epsilon}^{\top}
such that
\left\|{\bm{U}}_{\alpha,\epsilon}-\overline{{\bm{U}}}\right\|_{2}=O(\epsilon)
, e.g., we can find an arbitrary factorization and then right-multiply an orthogonal matrix so that the row vector with the largest norm aligns with the direction of
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
. Applying
Lemma

G.1
, we know that gradient flow starting with
{\bm{U}}_{\alpha,\epsilon}
converges to a point that is only
O(\epsilon^{2(1-\mu)})
far from
\bar{{\bm{u}}}
. So we have

\left\|\lim_{t\to+\infty}\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T({\bm{W}}_{\alpha})+t)-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{1}\right\|_{2}=O(\epsilon^{2(1-\mu)}).

Taking
\epsilon\to 0
complete the proof.
∎

G.3
Proof for
Theorem

5.11

Theorem G.2
.

Let
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
be a critical point of (
2
) satisfying that
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
is a local minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d,\leq r}
for some
r\geq 1
but not a minimizer in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
. Let
-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mu_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}{\bm{v}}_{i}^{\top}
be the eigendecomposition of
-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}})
. If
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}
, the following limit exists and is a solution of (
2
).

{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}(t):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\phi\left(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}+\epsilon{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top},\frac{1}{2\mu_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+t\right).

For
\{{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}\}\subseteq{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
, if there exists time
T_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
for every
\alpha
so that
\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})
converges to
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
with positive alignment with the top principal component
{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}
as
\alpha\to 0
, then
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\phi\left({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2\mu_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>}+t\right)={\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}(t).

Moreover, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left\|\phi\left({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2\mu_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>}+t\right)-{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}(t)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq C\left\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{\frac{\tilde{\gamma}}{2\mu_{1}+\tilde{\gamma}}}

for every sufficiently small
\alpha
, where
\tilde{\gamma}:=2\mu_{1}-\max\{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},0\}
.

Proof.

Following
Section

G.1
, we view
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))
as a dynamical system.

\frac{\textup{{d}}}{\textup{{d}}t}\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))=\tilde{{\bm{g}}}(\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))).

Let
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}=\overline{{\bm{U}}}\overline{{\bm{U}}}^{\top}
be a factorization of
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
, where
\overline{{\bm{U}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. Since
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
is a local minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d,\leq r}
,
\overline{{\bm{U}}}
is also a local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}\to\mathbb{R},{\bm{U}}\mapsto\frac{1}{2}f({\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top})
. Since
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
is not a minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
, by
Lemma

F.1
,
\overline{{\bm{U}}}
is full-rank. By
Theorem

F.5
,
{\bm{J}}(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}})
has eigenvalues
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j},\xi_{p},0
. By a similar argument as in
Section

G.1
, the Jacobian of
\tilde{{\bm{g}}}
at
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{W}}(t))
has eigenvalues
\mu_{i}+\mu_{j},\xi_{p}
.

Since
\overline{{\bm{U}}}
is a local minimizer,
\xi_{p}\leq 0
for all
p
. If
\mu_{1}>\mu_{2}
, then
2\mu_{1}
is the unique largest eigenvalue, and
Theorem

F.5
shows that
\mathrm{vec}_{\mathrm{LT}}({\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top})
is a left eigenvector associated with
2\mu_{1}
. The eigenvalue gap
\tilde{\gamma}:=2\mu_{1}-\max\{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\max\{\xi_{p}:1\leq p\leq rd-\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\}\}\geq 2\mu_{1}-\max\{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},0\}
.

Also note that
\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}},{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>=\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>
because
\left<\overline{{}{\bm{W}}},{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>=0
by (
29
). If
\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})
converges to
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
as
\alpha\to 0
, then it has positive alignment with
{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}
iff
\liminf_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha}),{\bm{v}}_{1}{\bm{v}}_{1}^{\top}\right>}{\left\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T_{\alpha})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}>0
. Then it is easy to translate
Theorem

5.3
to
Theorem

G.2
.
∎

G.4
Gradient Flow only finds minimizers (Proof for
Theorem

5.10
)

The proof for
Theorem

5.10
is based on the following two theorems from the literature.

Theorem G.3

(Theorem 3.1 in
Du and Lee 2018
)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
convex function. Then
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{L}({\bm{U}})=f({\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top}),k\geq d
satisfies that (1). Every local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}
is also a global minimizer; (2). All saddles are strict. Here saddles denote those stationary points whose hessian are not positive semi-definite (thus including local maximizers).
3
3

3

Though the original theorem is proven for convex functions of form
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell({\bm{x}}_{i}{\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top}{\bm{x}}_{i}^{\top},y_{i})
, where
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
convex for its first variable. By scrutinizing their proof, we can see the assumption can be relaxed to
f
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
convex.

Theorem G.4

(Theorem 2 in
Lee et al. 2017
)
.

Let
{\bm{g}}
be a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
mapping from
{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
and
\det(D{\bm{g}}(x))\neq 0
for all
{\bm{x}}\in{\mathcal{X}}
. Then the set of initial points that converge to an unstable fixed point has measure zero,
\mu\left(\{{\bm{x}}_{0}:\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bm{g}}^{k}({\bm{x}}_{0})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\bm{g}}^{*}\}\right)=0
, where
{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{\bm{g}}=\{{\bm{x}}:{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}})={\bm{x}},\max_{i}|\lambda_{i}(D{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}}))|>1\}
.

Theorem G.5

(GF only finds minimizers, a continuous analog of
Theorem

G.4
)
.

Let
{\bm{f}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-smooth function, and
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the solution of the following differential equation,

\frac{\textup{{d}}\phi({\bm{x}},t)}{\textup{{d}}t}={\bm{f}}(\phi({\bm{x}},t)),\quad\phi({\bm{x}},0)={\bm{x}},\quad\forall{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},t\in\mathbb{R}.

Then the set of initial points that converge to a unstable critical point has measure zero,
\mu\left(\left\{{\bm{x}}_{0}:\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi({\bm{x}}_{0},t)\in{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{\bm{f}}\right\}\right)=0
, where
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{\bm{f}}=\{{\bm{x}}:{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}})={\bm{0}},\lambda_{1}(D{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}}))>0\}
and
D{\bm{f}}
is the Jacobian matrix of
{\bm{f}}
.

Proof of
Theorem

G.5
.

By Theorem 1 in Section 2.3,
Perko 2013
, we know
\phi(\cdot,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-smooth for both
x,t
. We let
{\bm{g}}(x)=\phi(x,1)
, then we know
{\bm{g}}^{-1}(x)=\phi(x,-1)
and both
{\bm{g}},{\bm{g}}^{-1}
are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-smooth. Note that
D{\bm{g}}^{-1}(x)
is the inverse matrix of
D{\bm{g}}(x)
. So both of the two matrices are invertible. Thus we can apply
Theorem

G.4
and we know
\mu\left(\{x_{0}:\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bm{g}}^{k}(x_{0})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\bm{g}}^{*}\}\right)=0
.

Note that if
\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi(x,t)
exists, then
\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bm{g}}^{k}({\bm{x}})=\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi({\bm{x}},t)
. It remains to show that
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{\bm{f}}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{\bm{g}}
. For
{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}}_{0})={\bm{0}}
, we have
\phi({\bm{x}}_{0},t)={\bm{x}}_{0}
and thus
{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}}_{0})={\bm{x}}_{0}
. Now it suffices to prove that
\lambda_{1}(D{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}}_{0}))>1
. For every
t\in[0,1]
, by Corollary of Theorem 1 in Section 2.3,
Perko 2013
, we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}D\phi({\bm{x}},t)=D{\bm{f}}(\phi({\bm{x}},t))D\phi({\bm{x}},t)
,
\forall{\bm{x}},t
. Thus,

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}D\phi(x_{0},t)=D{\bm{f}}(\phi(x_{0},t))D\phi(x_{0},t)=D{\bm{f}}(x_{0})D\phi(x_{0},t).

Solving this ODE gives
D{\bm{g}}({\bm{x}}_{0})=D\phi({\bm{x}},1)=e^{D{\bm{f}}(x_{0})}D\phi({\bm{x}},0)=e^{D{\bm{f}}(x_{0})}
, where the last equality is due to
D\phi({\bm{x}},0)\equiv{\bm{I}},\ \forall{\bm{x}}
. Combining this with
\lambda_{1}(D{\bm{f}}(x_{0}))>0
, we have
\lambda_{1}(D{\bm{g}}(x_{0}))>1
.

Thus we have
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}_{\bm{f}}:=\{{\bm{x}}_{0}:{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}}_{0})={\bm{0}},\lambda_{1}(D{\bm{f}}({\bm{x}}_{0}))>0\}\subseteq{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{\bm{g}}
, which implies that
\{{\bm{x}}_{0}:\lim_{t\to\infty}\phi({\bm{x}}_{0},t)\in{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\}\subseteq\{{\bm{x}}_{0}:\lim_{k\to\infty}{\bm{g}}^{k}({\bm{x}}_{0})\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\bm{g}}^{*}\}

∎

See
5.10

Proof of
Theorem

5.10
.

For (1), by
Theorem

G.3
, we immediately know all the stationary points of
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
are either global minimizers or strict saddles. (2) is just a direct consequence of
Theorem

G.5
by setting
{\bm{f}}
in the above proof to
-\nabla\mathcal{L}
.
∎

Appendix H
Equivalence Between GF and GLRL

In this section we elaborate on the theoretical evidence that GF and GLRL are equivalent generically, including the case where GLRL does not end in the first phase. The word “generically” used when we want to assume one of the following regularity conditions:

1.

We want to assume that GF converges to a local minimizer (i.e., GF does not get stuck on saddle points);

2.

We want to assume that the top eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f({\bm{W}}))
is unique for a critical point
{\bm{W}}
of (
2
) that is not a minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
;

3.

We want to assume that a convergent sequence of PSD matrices
{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}\to\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
has positive alignment with
{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}
for some fixed vector
{\bm{v}}
with
\langle\overline{{}{\bm{W}}},{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\rangle={\bm{0}}
, i.e., for a convergent sequence of PSD matrices
{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}\to\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}
, it holds for sure that
\liminf\limits_{\alpha\to 0}\left<\frac{{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}}{\|{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}},{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\right>=\liminf\limits_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{\left<{\bm{W}}_{\alpha},{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}\right>}{\|{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}\geq 0
, and we further assume that the inequality is strict generically.

Theorem

G.2
uncovers how GF with infinitesimal initialization generically behaves. Let
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{0}:={\bm{0}}
. For every
r\geq 1
, if
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}
is a local minimizer in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d,\leq r-1}
but not a minimizer in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
, then
\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}))>0
by
Lemma

A.2
. Generically, the top eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}))
should be unique, i.e.,
\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}))>\lambda_{2}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}))
. This enables us to apply
Theorem

G.2
and deduce that the limiting trajectory

{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{r}(t):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\phi\left(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}+\epsilon{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top},\frac{1}{2\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}))}\log\frac{1}{\epsilon}+t\right)

exists, where
{\bm{u}}_{r}
is the top eigenvector of
-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1})
. This
{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{r}(\,\cdot\,)
is exactly the trajectory of GLRL in phase
r
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Note that
{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{r}(\,\cdot\,)
corresponds to a trajectory of GF minimizing
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
, which should generically converge to a local minimizer of
\mathcal{L}(\,\cdot\,)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times r}
. This means the limit
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}:=\lim_{t\to+\infty}{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{r}(t)
should generically be a local minimizer of
f(\,\cdot\,)
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d,\leq r}
. If
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
is further a minimizer in
{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
, then
\lambda_{1}(-\nabla f(\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}))\leq 0
and GLRL exits with
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
; otherwise GLRL enters phase
r+1
.

If GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
r
(defined below), then by
Theorem

G.2
, as
\alpha\to 0
, the minimum distance from GF to
{\bm{W}}^{\mathrm{G}}_{r}(t)
converges to
0
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, GF can get arbitrarily close to the
r
-th critical point
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
of GLRL, i.e., there exists a suitable choice
T^{(r)}_{\alpha}
so that
\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})=\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
. Note that
\left<\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r},{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top}\right>=0
by (
29
) and thus
\liminf\limits_{\alpha\to 0}\left<\frac{\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}}{\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{F}}},{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top}\right>=\liminf\limits_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{\left<\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha}),{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top}\right>}{\|\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})-\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}\geq 0
. Generically, there should exist a suitable choice of
T^{(r)}_{\alpha}
so that
\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})
not only converges to
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
but also has positive alignment with
{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top}
, that is, GF should generically align well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
r+1
.

Definition H.1
.

We say that GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
r
if there exists
T^{(r)}_{\alpha}
for every
\alpha>0
such that
\phi({\bm{W}}_{\alpha},T^{(r)}_{\alpha})
converges to
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r-1}
with positive alignment with
{\bm{u}}_{r}{\bm{u}}_{r}^{\top}
as
\alpha\to 0
.

If the initialization satisfies that
{\bm{W}}_{\alpha}
converges to
{\bm{0}}
with positive alignment with
{\bm{u}}_{1}{\bm{u}}_{1}^{\top}
as
\alpha\to 0
, then GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
1
, which can be seen by taking
T_{\alpha}^{(1)}=0
. Now assume that GF aligns well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
r-1
, then the above argument shows that GF should generically align well with GLRL in the beginning of phase
r
, if GLRL does not exit in phase
r-1
. In the other case, we can use a similar argument as in
Theorem

5.8
to show that GF converges to a solution near the minimizer
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
of
f(\,\cdot\,)
as
t\to\infty
, and the distance between the solution and
\overline{{}{\bm{W}}}_{r}
converges to
0
as
\alpha\to 0
. By this induction we prove that GF with infinitesimal initialization is equivalent to GLRL generically.

Appendix I
Proofs for Deep Matrix Factorization

I.1
Preliminary Lemmas

Lemma I.1
.

If
{\bm{W}}(0)\succeq{\bm{0}}
, then
{\bm{W}}(t)\succeq{\bm{0}}
and
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t))=\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(0))
for all
t
.

Proof.

Note that we can always find a set of balanced
{\bm{U}}_{i}(t)
,
such that
{\bm{U}}_{1}(t)\ldots{\bm{U}}_{L}(t)={\bm{W}}(t)
,
d_{2}=d_{3}=\cdots=d_{L}=\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t))
and write the dynamics of
{\bm{W}}(t)
in the space of
\{{\bm{U}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{L}
.
Thus it is clear that for all
t^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t^{\prime}))\leq\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t))
.
We can apply the same argument for
t^{\prime}
and we know
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t))\leq\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t^{\prime}))
. Thus
\mathrm{rank}({\bm{W}}(t))
is constant over time, and we denote it by
k
. Since eigenvalues are continuous matrix functions, and
\forall t,\lambda_{i}({\bm{W}}(t)),\ i\in[k]\neq 0
. Thus they cannot change their signs and it must hold that
{\bm{W}}(t)\succeq{\bm{0}}
.
∎

Lemma I.2
.

\forall a,b,P\in\mathbb{R}
, if
a>b\geq 0,P\geq 1
, then
\frac{a^{P}-b^{P}}{a-b}\leq Pa^{P-1}
.

Proof.

Let
f(x)=P(1-x)-(1-x^{P})
. Since
f^{\prime}(x)=-P+Px^{P-1}<0
for all
x\in[0,1)
,
f(x)\geq f(0)=0
. Then substituting
x
by
\frac{b}{a}
completes the proof.
∎

Recall we use
D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})[{\bm{M}}]
to denote the directional derivative along
{\bm{M}}
of
{\bm{F}}
at
{\bm{N}}
.

Lemma I.3
.

Let
{\bm{F}}:{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}\to{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d},{\bm{M}}\mapsto{\bm{M}}^{P}
, where
P\geq 1
and
P\in\mathbb{Q}
.
Then
\forall{\bm{M}},{\bm{N}}\succeq{\bm{0}}
,

\left\|D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})[{\bm{M}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq P\left\|{\bm{N}}\right\|_{2}^{P-1}\left\|{\bm{M}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}},

where
D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})[{\bm{M}}]:=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}+t{\bm{M}})-{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})}{t}
is the directional derivative of
{\bm{F}}
along
{\bm{M}}
.

Proof.

Let
{\bm{N}}={\bm{U}}{\bm{\Sigma}}{\bm{U}}^{\top}
, where
{\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top}={\bm{I}}
and
{\bm{\Sigma}}=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{d})
.
Note that
{\bm{F}}({\bm{U}}{\bm{M}}{\bm{U}}^{\top})={\bm{U}}{\bm{F}}({\bm{M}}){\bm{U}}^{\top}
for any
{\bm{M}}\in{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left\|D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})[{\bm{M}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\left\|{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}+t{\bm{M}})-{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{t}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\left\|{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}}+t{\bm{U}}^{\top}{\bm{M}}{\bm{U}})-{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{t}

\displaystyle=\left\|D{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{U}}^{\top}{\bm{M}}{\bm{U}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}.

Therefore, it suffices to prove the lemma for the case where
{\bm{N}}
is diagonal, i.e.,
{\bm{N}}={\bm{\Sigma}}
.

Assume
P=\frac{q}{p}
, where
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\geq p>0
. Define
{\bm{G}}({\bm{N}})={\bm{N}}^{\frac{1}{p}}
. Then
{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})^{p}={\bm{\Sigma}}
. Taking directional derivative on both sides along direction
{\bm{M}}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{p}{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})^{i-1}D{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})^{p-1}={\bm{M}},

So we have

[D{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]]_{ij}=\frac{m_{ij}}{\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sigma_{i}^{\frac{k-1}{p}}\sigma_{j}^{\frac{p-k}{p}}}.

Let
{\bm{H}}({\bm{G}})={\bm{G}}^{q}
. With the same argument, we know

[D{\bm{H}}({\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}}))[{\bm{M}}]]_{ij}=m_{ij}\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sigma_{i}^{\frac{k-1}{p}}\sigma_{j}^{\frac{q-k}{p}}.

Note that
{\bm{H}}({\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}}))={\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})
. By chain rule, we have

D{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]=D{\bm{H}}({\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}}))[D{\bm{G}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]].

That is,

[D{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]]_{ij}=m_{ij}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{q}\sigma_{i}^{\frac{k-1}{p}}\sigma_{j}^{\frac{q-k}{p}}}{\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sigma_{i}^{\frac{k-1}{p}}\sigma_{j}^{\frac{p-k}{p}}}.

When
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}
, clearly
[D{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]]_{ij}=m_{ij}\cdot\frac{q}{p}\cdot\sigma_{i}^{\frac{q-p}{p}}=Pm_{ij}\sigma_{i}^{P-1}
. Otherwise, we assume WLOG that
\sigma_{i}>\sigma_{j}
, we multiply
\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j}
to both numerator and denominator and we have

\left\lvert[D{\bm{F}}({\bm{\Sigma}})[{\bm{M}}]]_{ij}\right\rvert=\left\lvert m_{ij}\right\rvert\frac{\sigma_{i}^{P}-\sigma_{j}^{P}}{\sigma_{i}-\sigma_{j}}\leq\left\lvert m_{ij}\right\rvert P\sigma_{i}^{P-1}\leq\left\lvert m_{ij}\right\rvert P\left\|{\bm{\Sigma}}\right\|_{2}^{P-1}.

where the first inequality is by
Lemma

I.2
. Thus we conclude the proof.
∎

Lemma I.4
.

For any
{\bm{A}},{\bm{B}}\succeq{\bm{0}}
and
P\in\mathbb{R},P\geq 1
,

\left\|{\bm{A}}^{P}-{\bm{B}}^{P}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq P\left\|{\bm{A}}-{\bm{B}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\max\left\{\left\|{\bm{A}}\right\|_{2}^{P-1},\left\|{\bm{B}}\right\|_{2}^{P-1}\right\}.

Proof.

Since both sides are continuous in
P
and
\mathbb{Q}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
, it suffices to prove the lemma for
P\in\mathbb{Q}
. Let
\rho:=\max\left\{\left\|{\bm{A}}\right\|_{2},\left\|{\bm{B}}\right\|_{2}\right\}
and
{\bm{F}}({\bm{M}})={\bm{M}}^{P}
.
Define
{\bm{N}}:[0,1]\to{\mathbb{S}}^{+}_{d}
,
{\bm{N}}(t)=(1-t){\bm{A}}+t{\bm{B}}
, we have

1.

\left\|{\bm{N}}(t)\right\|_{2}\leq\rho
, since
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
is convex.

2.

\left\|D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}(t))[{\bm{B}}-{\bm{A}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq P\left\|{\bm{N}}(t)\right\|_{2}^{P-1}\left\|{\bm{B}}-{\bm{A}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}
by
Lemma

I.3
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}(1))-{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}(0))\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\left\|\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}(t))}{\textup{{d}}t}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\textup{{d}}t

\displaystyle=\int_{t=0}^{1}\left\|D{\bm{F}}({\bm{N}}(t))[{\bm{B}}-{\bm{A}}]\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\textup{{d}}t

\displaystyle\leq P\left\|{\bm{A}}-{\bm{B}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\rho^{P-1},

which completes the proof.
∎

For a locally Lipschitz function
f(\,\cdot\,)
, the Clarke subdifferential
[
Clarke 1975
,
Clarke 1990
,
Clarke et al. 2008
]
of
f
at any point
{\bm{x}}
is the following convex set

\frac{\partial^{\circ}f({\bm{x}})}{\partial{\bm{x}}}:=\mathrm{co}\left\{\lim_{k\to\infty}\nabla f({\bm{x}}_{k}):{\bm{x}}_{k}\to{\bm{x}},f\text{ is differentiable at }{\bm{x}}_{k}\right\},

where
\mathrm{co}
denotes the convex hull.

Clarke subdifferential generalize the standard notion of gradients in the sense that, when
f
is smooth,
\frac{\partial^{\circ}f({\bm{x}})}{\partial{\bm{x}}}=\{\nabla f({\bm{x}})\}
. Clarke subdifferential satisfies the chain rule:

Theorem I.5

(Theorem 2.3.10,
Clarke 1990
)
.

Let
{\bm{F}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a differentiable function and
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
Lipschitz around
{\bm{F}}({\bm{x}})
. Then
f=g\circ{\bm{F}}
is Lipschitz around
{\bm{x}}
and one has

\frac{\partial^{\circ}f({\bm{x}})}{\partial{\bm{x}}}\subseteq\frac{\partial^{\circ}g({\bm{F}}({\bm{x}}))}{\partial{\bm{F}}}\circ\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{F}}({\bm{x}})}{\textup{{d}}{\bm{x}}}.

Let
\lambda_{m}:{\mathbb{S}}_{d}\to\mathbb{R},{\bm{M}}\mapsto\lambda_{m}({\bm{M}})
be the
m
-th largest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix
{\bm{M}}
. The following theorem gives the Clarke’s subdifferentials of the eigenvalue:

Theorem I.6

(Theorem 5.3,
Hiriart-Urruty and Lewis 1999
)
.

The Clarke subdifferential of the eigenvalue function
\lambda_{m}
is given below, where
\mathrm{co}
denotes the convex hull:

\frac{\partial^{\circ}\lambda_{m}({\bm{M}})}{\partial{\bm{M}}}=\mathrm{co}\{{\bm{v}}{\bm{v}}^{\top}:{\bm{M}}{\bm{v}}=\lambda_{m}({\bm{M}}){\bm{v}},\left\|{\bm{v}}\right\|_{2}=1\}.

I.2
Proof of
Lemma

6.1

Since
{\bm{W}}(t)\succeq{\bm{0}}
by
Lemma

I.1
, (
11
) can be rewritten as the following:

\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{W}}}{\textup{{d}}t}=-\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}{\bm{W}}^{\frac{2i}{L}}\nabla f({\bm{W}}){\bm{W}}^{2-\frac{2i+2}{L}}.

(31)

Proof for
Lemma

6.1
.

Suppose
{\bm{W}}(t)
is a symmetric solution of (
11
). By
Lemma

I.1
, we know
{\bm{W}}(t)
also satisfies (
31
).
Now we let
{\bm{R}}(t)
be the solution of the following ODE with
{\bm{R}}(0):=({\bm{W}}(0))^{\frac{1}{L}}
.
Note we don’t define
{\bm{R}}(t)
by
({\bm{W}}(t))^{\frac{1}{L}}
.

\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{R}}}{\textup{{d}}t}=-\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}(-1)^{i}{\bm{R}}^{i}\nabla f({\bm{R}}^{L}){\bm{R}}^{L-1-i}.

(32)

The calculation below shows that
{\bm{R}}^{L}(t)
also satisfies (
31
).

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{R}}^{L}}{\textup{{d}}t}=\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}{\bm{R}}^{j}\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{R}}}{\textup{{d}}t}{\bm{R}}^{L-1-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{L-1}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}(-1)^{i}{\bm{R}}^{i+j}\nabla f({\bm{R}}^{L}){\bm{R}}^{2L-2-i-j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{2L-2}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{i}(-1)^{j}\right){\bm{R}}^{i}\nabla f({\bm{R}}^{L}){\bm{R}}^{2T-2-i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}({\bm{R}}^{L})^{\frac{2i}{L}}\nabla f({\bm{R}}^{L})({\bm{R}}^{L})^{2-\frac{2+2i}{L}}.

Since
{\bm{R}}^{L}(0)={\bm{W}}(0)
, by existence and uniqueness theorem,
{\bm{R}}^{L}(t)={\bm{W}}(t)
,
\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
. So

\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{M}}}{\textup{{d}}t}={\bm{R}}\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{R}}}{dt}+\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{R}}}{dt}{\bm{R}}=-\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{L/2}){\bm{M}}^{L/2}-{\bm{M}}^{L/2}\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{L/2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

I.3
Proof for
Theorem

6.2

Now we turn to prove
Theorem

6.2
. Let
P=L/2
. Then (
12
) can be rewritten as

\frac{d{\bm{M}}}{dt}=-\left(\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}){\bm{M}}^{P}+{\bm{M}}^{P}\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P})\right).

(33)

The following lemma about the growth rate of
\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}})
is used later in the proof.

Lemma I.7
.

Suppose
{\bm{M}}(t)
satisfies (
33
), we have for any
T^{\prime}>T
, and
k\in[d]
,

\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(T^{\prime}))-\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(T))\leq\int_{T}^{T^{\prime}}2\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))^{P}\|\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\|_{2}\textup{{d}}t.

(34)

and

\frac{1}{P-1}\left(\lambda^{1-P}_{k}({\bm{M}}(T))-\lambda^{1-P}_{k}({\bm{M}}(T^{\prime}))\right)\leq\int_{T}^{T^{\prime}}2\|\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\|_{2}\textup{{d}}t.

(35)

Proof.

Since
\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))
is locally Lipschitz in
t
, by Rademacher’s theorem, we know
\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))
is differentiable almost everywhere, and the following holds

\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(T^{\prime}))-\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(T))=\int_{T}^{T^{\prime}}\frac{\textup{{d}}\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))}{\textup{{d}}t}\textup{{d}}t.

When
\frac{\textup{{d}}\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))}{\textup{{d}}t}
exists, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\textup{{d}}\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}(t))}{\textup{{d}}t}

\displaystyle\in\left\{\left<{\bm{G}},\frac{\textup{{d}}{\bm{M}}(t)}{\textup{{d}}t}\right>:{\bm{G}}\in\frac{\partial^{\circ}\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}})}{\partial{\bm{M}}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{2\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\left<{\bm{G}},-\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\right>:{\bm{G}}\in\frac{\partial^{\circ}\lambda_{k}({\bm{M}})}{\partial{\bm{M}}}\right\}

Note that
\left\|{\bm{G}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\left\|{\bm{G}}\right\|_{\ast}=1
. So
\left|\left<{\bm{G}},-\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\right>\right|\leq\|\nabla f({\bm{M}}^{P}(t))\|_{2}
. We can prove (
35
) with a similar argument.
∎

To prove
Theorem

6.2
, it suffices to consider the case that
{\bm{M}}(0)=\hat{\alpha}{\bm{I}}
where
\hat{\alpha}:=\alpha^{1/P}
. WLOG we can assume
-\nabla f({\bm{0}})=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{d})
by choosing a suitable standard basis. By assumption in
Theorem

6.2
, we have
\mu_{1}>\max\{\mu_{2},0\}
and
\mu_{1}=\left\|\nabla f({\bm{0}})\right\|_{2}
. We use
{\phi_{m}}({\bm{M}}_{0},t)
to denote the solution of
{\bm{M}}(t)
when
{\bm{M}}(0)={\bm{M}}_{0}
.

Let
R>0
. Since
f(\,\cdot\,)
is
\mathcal{C}^{3}
-smooth, there exists
\beta>0
such that

\left\|\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{1})-\nabla f({\bm{W}}_{2})\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\beta\left\|{\bm{W}}_{1}-{\bm{W}}_{2}\right\|_{2}

for all
{\bm{W}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{2}
with
